========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess the main thing is… I’m having a really hard time with anything that requires me to sit down and do sustained mental work. Like, at my job, I can handle a lot of moving parts in the moment, but when it comes to things like monthly reports or planning out a multi-site health event in a detailed, organized way, it’s like I hit a wall.

I know the work matters. I’m not confused about the importance of it, and I’m not trying to avoid responsibility. But I keep putting those kinds of tasks off, sometimes until there’s real pressure, and the whole time it’s sitting in the back of my mind making me feel awful. It’s especially the stuff that takes focused brain effort for a long stretch — sitting there, sorting details, writing, planning, making decisions in sequence. I’ll mean to start, and then I just… don’t, or I start and stall out almost immediately.

That’s really what brought me in. It’s starting to feel like more than just being busy or not liking paperwork.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… yes, I think so. Looking back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school. The way it was always described to my parents was, you know, “He’s bright, he understands the material,” but then there was always a “but.” Like, “but he’s daydreaming,” or “but he’s not keeping track of his papers,” or “but he didn’t turn in the worksheet.”

I remember losing worksheets constantly — or not even losing them exactly, just having them disappear into my desk or backpack somehow. And homework was a whole production at home. My mother basically had to check my backpack every night, because if she didn’t, I’d forget something or not realize there was an assignment due, or I’d do it and then not turn it in. That happened enough that it became kind of a routine, her going through my folder and asking, “What’s this? Was this supposed to be handed in?” And half the time I’d be like, “Oh… yeah.”

So it wasn’t that I couldn’t learn the stuff. I think that’s what made it confusing for everyone. It was more that the follow-through — keeping the paper, remembering it, getting it back to school, actually handing it in — that whole chain was where things kept falling apart.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F90.2, F41.9, F32.9, F34.1, F42.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess the main thing is… I’m having a really hard time with anything that requires me to sit down and do sustained mental work. Like, at my job, I can handle a lot of moving parts in the moment, but when it comes to things like monthly reports or planning out a multi-site health event in a detailed, organized way, it’s like I hit a wall.\n\nI know the work matters. I’m not confused about the importance of it, and I’m not trying to avoid responsibility. But I keep putting those kinds of tasks off, sometimes until there’s real pressure, and the whole time it’s sitting in the back of my mind making me feel awful. It’s especially the stuff that takes focused brain effort for a long stretch — sitting there, sorting details, writing, planning, making decisions in sequence. I’ll mean to start, and then I just… don’t, or I start and stall out almost immediately.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. It’s starting to feel like more than just being busy or not liking paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble with schoolwork or homework when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Looking back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school. The way it was always described to my parents was, you know, “He’s bright, he understands the material,” but then there was always a “but.” Like, “but he’s daydreaming,” or “but he’s not keeping track of his papers,” or “but he didn’t turn in the worksheet.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets constantly — or not even losing them exactly, just having them disappear into my desk or backpack somehow. And homework was a whole production at home. My mother basically had to check my backpack every night, because if she didn’t, I’d forget something or not realize there was an assignment due, or I’d do it and then not turn it in. That happened enough that it became kind of a routine, her going through my folder and asking, “What’s this? Was this supposed to be handed in?” And half the time I’d be like, “Oh… yeah.”\n\nSo it wasn’t that I couldn’t learn the stuff. I think that’s what made it confusing for everyone. for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve been forgetting to return calls from volunteers, missing small errands for events, and once or twice I’ve had to apologize because a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I notice I start talking too much when I’m nervous about having dropped the ball, like I’ll over-explain a scheduling mistake and keep going even after the other person clearly got the point.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers told my parents I was bright but always daydreaming, losing worksheets, and forgetting to turn in homework unless my mother checked my backpack every night.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still getting by at work and in the community, but people have had to cover gaps for me, deadlines have slipped, and I’ve embarrassed myself with last-minute apologies to coworkers and friends.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 11 months, since my friend died and I took on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S006\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess the main thing is… I’m having a really hard time with anything that requires me to sit down and do sustained mental work. Like, at my job, I can handle a lot of moving parts in the moment, but when it comes to things like monthly reports or planning out a multi-site health event in a detailed, organized way, it’s like I hit a wall.\n\nI know the work matters. I’m not confused about the importance of it, and I’m not trying to avoid responsibility. But I keep putting those kinds of tasks off, sometimes until there’s real pressure, and the whole time it’s sitting in the back of my mind making me feel awful. It’s especially the stuff that takes focused brain effort for a long stretch — sitting there, sorting details, writing, planning, making decisions in sequence. I’ll mean to start, and then I just… don’t, or I start and stall out almost immediately.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. It’s starting to feel like more than just being busy or not liking paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble with schoolwork or homework when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Looking back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school. The way it was always described to my parents was, you know, “He’s bright, he understands the material,” but then there was always a “but.” Like, “but he’s daydreaming,” or “but he’s not keeping track of his papers,” or “but he didn’t turn in the worksheet.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets constantly — or not even losing them exactly, just having them disappear into my desk or backpack somehow. And homework was a whole production at home. My mother basically had to check my backpack every night, because if she didn’t, I’d forget something or not realize there was an assignment due, or I’d do it and then not turn it in. That happened enough that it became kind of a routine, her going through my folder and asking, “What’s this? Was this supposed to be handed in?” And half the time I’d be like, “Oh… yeah.”\n\nSo it wasn’t that I couldn’t learn the stuff. I think that’s what made it confusing for everyone. It was more that the follow-through — keeping the paper, remembering it, getting it back to school, actually handing it in — that whole chain was where things kept falling apart.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… that’s a little harder for me to say. I don’t really remember being the kid who was constantly jumping out of his seat or running around the classroom when I wasn’t supposed to. At least, that’s not the part that stands out in my memory, and I don’t remember teachers making that the main complaint.\n\nI mean, maybe I shifted around or doodled or messed with things at my desk like any kid, but I don’t have a clear memory of being unable to stay seated. The stuff I remember more is kind of different from that, but in terms of being physically fidgety or always up and moving, I’m not sure I could honestly say that was a big issue.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs an adult, do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to relax or stay still inside?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as physically restless in the obvious way, like I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit through a meeting. I mean, I can sit still if I need to.

The “inside” part is harder. Sometimes I feel mentally wound up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness exactly or more just stress from knowing I’m behind on something or that I’ve got things hanging over me. I wouldn’t say relaxing comes naturally to me all the time, but I also don’t have a clear sense of being driven by a motor or needing to constantly move.

So… maybe a little at times, but it’s not one of the main things I would have thought to mention. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by restless, because I’m not sure if what I experience counts.

It was more that the follow-through — keeping the paper, remembering it, getting it back to school, actually handing it in — that whole chain was where things kept falling apart.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… that’s a little harder for me to say. I don’t really remember being the kid who was constantly jumping out of his seat or running around the classroom when I wasn’t supposed to. At least, that’s not the part that stands out in my memory, and I don’t remember teachers making that the main complaint.\n\nI mean, maybe I shifted around or doodled or messed with things at my desk like any kid, but I don’t have a clear memory of being unable to stay seated. The stuff I remember more is kind of different from that, but in terms of being physically fidgety or always up and moving, I’m not sure I could honestly say that was a big issue.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to relax or stay still inside?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as physically restless in the obvious way, like I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit through a meeting. I mean, I can sit still if I need to.\n\nThe “inside” part is harder. Sometimes I feel mentally wound up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness exactly or more just stress from knowing I’m behind on something or that I’ve got things hanging over me. I wouldn’t say relaxing comes naturally to me all the time, but I also don’t have a clear sense of being driven by a motor or needing to constantly move.\n\nSo… maybe a little at times, but it’s not one of the main things I would have thought to mention. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by restless, because I’m not sure if what I experience counts.\n\n[Last Question]\nAs an adult, do you often make careless mistakes or miss details in your work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I do that more than I’d like, and it’s frustrating because a lot of the time I genuinely think I checked it.

Like at work, I might send out a clinic staffing schedule and feel like I looked it over — names, dates, coverage, all of that — and then someone replies and says, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Wednesday?” or “This person isn’t at that location,” or there’s a missing detail that should have been obvious. And it’s not because I don’t care. I really do care, especially with work stuff, because it affects other people. But somehow my eyes just kind of pass over the mistake.

It happens outside of work too. I help with emails for my soccer league sometimes, and I’ll send something out thinking it’s complete, and then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left out some detail that everyone needs. And then I feel embarrassed because it’s the kind of thing where I think, “How did I not see that?” I mean, I had it right in front of me.

So, yeah, careless mistakes or missed details are definitely a thing for me. Not every single thing I do, but often enough that it’s become a pattern, and I’ve had to try to double-check more than once because one pass doesn’t always catch it.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.

It could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll have a random thought pop in, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the soccer league,” and then my brain sort of follows that thread.

By the time I catch myself and come back to the meeting, I’ve missed a few minutes, and then I’m trying to piece together what was said from context. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. So yes, distractions from both around me and my own thoughts are a pretty regular problem when I’m trying to focus.

I’d probably need you to define what you mean by restless, because I’m not sure if what I experience counts.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often make careless mistakes or miss details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I do that more than I’d like, and it’s frustrating because a lot of the time I genuinely think I checked it.\n\nLike at work, I might send out a clinic staffing schedule and feel like I looked it over — names, dates, coverage, all of that — and then someone replies and says, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Wednesday?” or “This person isn’t at that location,” or there’s a missing detail that should have been obvious. And it’s not because I don’t care. I really do care, especially with work stuff, because it affects other people. But somehow my eyes just kind of pass over the mistake.\n\nIt happens outside of work too. I help with emails for my soccer league sometimes, and I’ll send something out thinking it’s complete, and then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left out some detail that everyone needs. And then I feel embarrassed because it’s the kind of thing where I think, “How did I not see that?” I mean, I had it right in front of me.\n\nSo, yeah, careless mistakes or missed details are definitely a thing for me. Not every single thing I do, but often enough that it’s become a pattern, and I’ve had to try to double-check more than once because one pass doesn’t always catch it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to do a task that needs focus, do you often get distracted by your own thoughts or things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.\n\nIt could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll have a random thought pop in, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the soccer league,” and then my brain sort of follows that thread.\n\nBy the time I catch myself and come back to the meeting, I’ve missed a few minutes, and then I’m trying to piece together what was said from context. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. So yes, distractions from both around me and my own thoughts are a pretty regular problem when I’m trying to focus.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you have recurrent unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I have thoughts that get stuck sometimes, but usually they’re more like worries or reminders — like, “Did I forget to send that?” or “I need to follow up on this” — not thoughts that feel bizarre or scary or totally unwanted in that intrusive way.

I can get mentally caught up in stuff, especially if there’s something unresolved, but it feels more like my mind jumping back to responsibilities than having unwanted thoughts I can’t dismiss.

So I guess my answer would be: not really, or at least not in the way I think you mean.

You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S001\n  name: Detail_Inattention_Errors\n  description: Often fails to give close attention to details or makes careless mistakes.\n  manifestation: I’ll send out a clinic staffing schedule or a soccer league email thinking I checked it, and then someone points out I put the wrong date, left off a location, or missed a detail that I would’ve caught before.\n- key: S005\n  name: Organization_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty organizing tasks and activities.\n  manifestation: When I’m juggling work projects, memorial committee stuff, and outreach events, I have notes in three different places and I can’t seem to figure out what needs to happen first, so I end up rushing at the end.\n- key: S006\n  name: Sustained_Mental_Effort_Avoidance\n  description: Often avoids, dislikes, or is reluctant to engage in tasks that require sustained mental effort.\n  manifestation: The main reason I’m here is that anything that takes real sitting-down brain work, like monthly reports or planning a multi-site health event, feels like I hit a wall and I keep putting it off even when I know it matters.\n- key: S008\n  name: Distractibility\n  description: Often easily distracted by extraneous stimuli (for older adolescents and adults, may include unrelated thoughts).\n  manifestation: In meetings, I’ll be trying to listen to a budget update, but a phone vibrating, people talking in the hall, or even a thought about something I forgot for the league pulls me away and I miss the next few minutes.\n- key: S009\n  name: Daily_Activities_Forgetfulness\n  description: Often forgetful in daily activities (e.g., doing chores, running errands; for older adolescents and adults, returning calls, paying bills, keeping appointments).\n  manifestation: I’ve been forgetting to return calls from volunteers, missing small errands for events, and once or twice I’ve had to apologize because a community meeting completely slipped my mind until someone texted me.\n- key: S015\n  name: Excessive_Talking\n  description: Often talks excessively.\n  manifestation: I notice I start talking too much when I’m nervous about having dropped the ball, like I’ll over-explain a scheduling mistake and keep going even after the other person clearly got the point.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: Even in elementary school, my teachers told my parents I was bright but always daydreaming, losing worksheets, and forgetting to turn in homework unless my mother checked my backpack every night.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’m still getting by at work and in the community, but people have had to cover gaps for me, deadlines have slipped, and I’ve embarrassed myself with last-minute apologies to coworkers and friends.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about 1 year and 11 months, since my friend died and I took on more of the league and outreach responsibilities.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S006\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often make careless mistakes or miss details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I do that more than I’d like, and it’s frustrating because a lot of the time I genuinely think I checked it.\n\nLike at work, I might send out a clinic staffing schedule and feel like I looked it over — names, dates, coverage, all of that — and then someone replies and says, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Wednesday?” or “This person isn’t at that location,” or there’s a missing detail that should have been obvious. And it’s not because I don’t care. I really do care, especially with work stuff, because it affects other people. But somehow my eyes just kind of pass over the mistake.\n\nIt happens outside of work too. I help with emails for my soccer league sometimes, and I’ll send something out thinking it’s complete, and then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left out some detail that everyone needs. And then I feel embarrassed because it’s the kind of thing where I think, “How did I not see that?” I mean, I had it right in front of me.\n\nSo, yeah, careless mistakes or missed details are definitely a thing for me. Not every single thing I do, but often enough that it’s become a pattern, and I’ve had to try to double-check more than once because one pass doesn’t always catch it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to do a task that needs focus, do you often get distracted by your own thoughts or things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.\n\nIt could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll have a random thought pop in, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the soccer league,” and then my brain sort of follows that thread.\n\nBy the time I catch myself and come back to the meeting, I’ve missed a few minutes, and then I’m trying to piece together what was said from context. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. So yes, distractions from both around me and my own thoughts are a pretty regular problem when I’m trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurrent unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I have thoughts that get stuck sometimes, but usually they’re more like worries or reminders — like, “Did I forget to send that?” or “I need to follow up on this” — not thoughts that feel bizarre or scary or totally unwanted in that intrusive way.\n\nWhen you say “intrusive,” I think of something that just keeps forcing its way in and feels really disturbing or hard to shake, and I don’t think that’s a major thing for me. I can get mentally caught up in stuff, especially if there’s something unresolved, but it feels more like my mind jumping back to responsibilities than having unwanted thoughts I can’t dismiss.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: not really, or at least not in the way I think you mean.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo people often tell you that you seem not to listen when they’re speaking to you directly?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think people say that to me in those exact words very often, like “you’re not listening.” At least not that I can immediately think of.

Sometimes someone might check in and say, “Did you hear what I said?” or “Does that make sense?” but I don’t know if that’s because I seemed checked out or just because they wanted a response from me. I may need to think about that a little more, because I’m not always the best judge of how I come across in the moment.

So I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed people often telling me that directly, no. If anything, it’s more something I’d have to ask people close to me about to know for sure.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess the main thing is… I’m having a really hard time with anything that requires me to sit down and do sustained mental work. Like, at my job, I can handle a lot of moving parts in the moment, but when it comes to things like monthly reports or planning out a multi-site health event in a detailed, organized way, it’s like I hit a wall.\n\nI know the work matters. I’m not confused about the importance of it, and I’m not trying to avoid responsibility. But I keep putting those kinds of tasks off, sometimes until there’s real pressure, and the whole time it’s sitting in the back of my mind making me feel awful. It’s especially the stuff that takes focused brain effort for a long stretch — sitting there, sorting details, writing, planning, making decisions in sequence. I’ll mean to start, and then I just… don’t, or I start and stall out almost immediately.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. It’s starting to feel like more than just being busy or not liking paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble with schoolwork or homework when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Looking back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school. The way it was always described to my parents was, you know, “He’s bright, he understands the material,” but then there was always a “but.” Like, “but he’s daydreaming,” or “but he’s not keeping track of his papers,” or “but he didn’t turn in the worksheet.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets constantly — or not even losing them exactly, just having them disappear into my desk or backpack somehow. And homework was a whole production at home. My mother basically had to check my backpack every night, because if she didn’t, I’d forget something or not realize there was an assignment due, or I’d do it and then not turn it in. That happened enough that it became kind of a routine, her going through my folder and asking, “What’s this? Was this supposed to be handed in?” And half the time I’d be like, “Oh… yeah.”\n\nSo it wasn’t that I couldn’t learn the stuff. I think that’s what made it confusing for everyone. It was more that the follow-through — keeping the paper, remembering it, getting it back to school, actually handing it in — that whole chain was where things kept falling apart.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… that’s a little harder for me to say. I don’t really remember being the kid who was constantly jumping out of his seat or running around the classroom when I wasn’t supposed to. At least, that’s not the part that stands out in my memory, and I don’t remember teachers making that the main complaint.\n\nI mean, maybe I shifted around or doodled or messed with things at my desk like any kid, but I don’t have a clear memory of being unable to stay seated. The stuff I remember more is kind of different from that, but in terms of being physically fidgety or always up and moving, I’m not sure I could honestly say that was a big issue.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to relax or stay still inside?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as physically restless in the obvious way, like I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit through a meeting. I mean, I can sit still if I need to.\n\nThe “inside” part is harder. Sometimes I feel mentally wound up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness exactly or more just stress from knowing I’m behind on something or that I’ve got things hanging over me. I wouldn’t say relaxing comes naturally to me all the time, but I also don’t have a clear sense of being driven by a motor or needing to constantly move.\n\nSo… maybe a little at times, but it’s not one of the main things I would have thought to mention. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by restless, because I’m not sure if what I experience counts.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often make careless mistakes or miss details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I do that more than I’d like, and it’s frustrating because a lot of the time I genuinely think I checked it.\n\nLike at work, I might send out a clinic staffing schedule and feel like I looked it over — names, dates, coverage, all of that — and then someone replies and says, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Wednesday?” or “This person isn’t at that location,” or there’s a missing detail that should have been obvious. And it’s not because I don’t care. I really do care, especially with work stuff, because it affects other people. But somehow my eyes just kind of pass over the mistake.\n\nIt happens outside of work too. I help with emails for my soccer league sometimes, and I’ll send something out thinking it’s complete, and then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left out some detail that everyone needs. And then I feel embarrassed because it’s the kind of thing where I think, “How did I not see that?” I mean, I had it right in front of me.\n\nSo, yeah, careless mistakes or missed details are definitely a thing for me. Not every single thing I do, but often enough that it’s become a pattern, and I’ve had to try to double-check more than once because one pass doesn’t always catch it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to do a task that needs focus, do you often get distracted by your own thoughts or things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.\n\nIt could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll have a random thought pop in, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the soccer league,” and then my brain sort of follows that thread.\n\nBy the time I catch myself and come back to the meeting, I’ve missed a few minutes, and then I’m trying to piece together what was said from context. I help with emails for my soccer league sometimes, and I’ll send something out thinking it’s complete, and then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left out some detail that everyone needs. And then I feel embarrassed because it’s the kind of thing where I think, “How did I not see that?” I mean, I had it right in front of me.\n\nSo, yeah, careless mistakes or missed details are definitely a thing for me. Not every single thing I do, but often enough that it’s become a pattern, and I’ve had to try to double-check more than once because one pass doesn’t always catch it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to do a task that needs focus, do you often get distracted by your own thoughts or things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.\n\nIt could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll have a random thought pop in, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the soccer league,” and then my brain sort of follows that thread.\n\nBy the time I catch myself and come back to the meeting, I’ve missed a few minutes, and then I’m trying to piece together what was said from context. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. So yes, distractions from both around me and my own thoughts are a pretty regular problem when I’m trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurrent unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I have thoughts that get stuck sometimes, but usually they’re more like worries or reminders — like, “Did I forget to send that?” or “I need to follow up on this” — not thoughts that feel bizarre or scary or totally unwanted in that intrusive way.\n\nWhen you say “intrusive,” I think of something that just keeps forcing its way in and feels really disturbing or hard to shake, and I don’t think that’s a major thing for me. I can get mentally caught up in stuff, especially if there’s something unresolved, but it feels more like my mind jumping back to responsibilities than having unwanted thoughts I can’t dismiss.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: not really, or at least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do people often tell you that you seem not to listen when they’re speaking to you directly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think people say that to me in those exact words very often, like “you’re not listening.” At least not that I can immediately think of.\n\nSometimes someone might check in and say, “Did you hear what I said?” or “Does that make sense?” but I don’t know if that’s because I seemed checked out or just because they wanted a response from me. I may need to think about that a little more, because I’m not always the best judge of how I come across in the moment.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed people often telling me that directly, no. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, that happens. Not every single time I read, but often enough that I notice it and it gets frustrating.

I’ll be reading something I actually need to understand — like a report or an email with details I’m supposed to follow — and my eyes keep moving down the page, but my mind has kind of left. It’s similar to what happens in meetings. I’ll be trying to stay with a budget update or some discussion, and then something small pulls me off — my phone vibrating, people talking out in the hall, or even just a thought like, “Oh, I forgot to do that thing for the league.” And then a few minutes are gone.

With reading, it’s more like I get to the end of a paragraph and realize I don’t really know what I just read. I saw the words, but I didn’t take them in. So I have to go back and read the same paragraph again, sometimes more than once, especially if there’s any distraction around me or if my mind starts chasing some unrelated thought. It’s annoying because I’m not trying to skim it or ignore it — I genuinely want to understand it — but I’ll catch myself after the fact and think, “Wait, I have no idea what that just said.”

That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.\n\nIt could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. I’m not sure I’d call it “long stretches,” no. I mean, I’ve had periods where I felt worn down or discouraged, or just kind of low for a bit, especially when things feel like they’re piling up. But when you say depressed most days for a long stretch, I don’t immediately think, yes, that’s been me.

I guess I’d want to clarify what you mean by long — like weeks, months? Because I can have bad days, or maybe a rough week where my mood isn’t great, but I don’t know that I’ve had a sustained period where I felt down or depressed most days in that more ongoing way.

So my best answer is: not really that I’ve noticed, at least not in a clear, prolonged way.

I mean, I can sit still if I need to.\n\nThe “inside” part is harder. I wouldn’t say relaxing comes naturally to me all the time, but I also don’t have a clear sense of being driven by a motor or needing to constantly move.\n\nSo… maybe a little at times, but it’s not one of the main things I would have thought to mention. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by restless, because I’m not sure if what I experience counts.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often make careless mistakes or miss details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I do that more than I’d like, and it’s frustrating because a lot of the time I genuinely think I checked it.\n\nLike at work, I might send out a clinic staffing schedule and feel like I looked it over — names, dates, coverage, all of that — and then someone replies and says, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Wednesday?” or “This person isn’t at that location,” or there’s a missing detail that should have been obvious. And it’s not because I don’t care. I really do care, especially with work stuff, because it affects other people. But somehow my eyes just kind of pass over the mistake.\n\nIt happens outside of work too. I help with emails for my soccer league sometimes, and I’ll send something out thinking it’s complete, and then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left out some detail that everyone needs. And then I feel embarrassed because it’s the kind of thing where I think, “How did I not see that?” I mean, I had it right in front of me.\n\nSo, yeah, careless mistakes or missed details are definitely a thing for me. Not every single thing I do, but often enough that it’s become a pattern, and I’ve had to try to double-check more than once because one pass doesn’t always catch it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to do a task that needs focus, do you often get distracted by your own thoughts or things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.\n\nIt could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll have a random thought pop in, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the soccer league,” and then my brain sort of follows that thread.\n\nBy the time I catch myself and come back to the meeting, I’ve missed a few minutes, and then I’m trying to piece together what was said from context. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. So yes, distractions from both around me and my own thoughts are a pretty regular problem when I’m trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurrent unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I have thoughts that get stuck sometimes, but usually they’re more like worries or reminders — like, “Did I forget to send that?” or “I need to follow up on this” — not thoughts that feel bizarre or scary or totally unwanted in that intrusive way.\n\nWhen you say “intrusive,” I think of something that just keeps forcing its way in and feels really disturbing or hard to shake, and I don’t think that’s a major thing for me. I can get mentally caught up in stuff, especially if there’s something unresolved, but it feels more like my mind jumping back to responsibilities than having unwanted thoughts I can’t dismiss.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: not really, or at least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do people often tell you that you seem not to listen when they’re speaking to you directly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think people say that to me in those exact words very often, like “you’re not listening.” At least not that I can immediately think of.\n\nSometimes someone might check in and say, “Did you hear what I said?” or “Does that make sense?” but I don’t know if that’s because I seemed checked out or just because they wanted a response from me. I may need to think about that a little more, because I’m not always the best judge of how I come across in the moment.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed people often telling me that directly, no. If anything, it’s more something I’d have to ask people close to me about to know for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you read something that you want to understand, do you often find yourself having to reread the same paragraph because your mind drifted off?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens. Not every single time I read, but often enough that I notice it and it gets frustrating.\n\nI’ll be reading something I actually need to understand — like a report or an email with details I’m supposed to follow — and my eyes keep moving down the page, but my mind has kind of left. It’s similar to what happens in meetings. I’ll be trying to stay with a budget update or some discussion, and then something small pulls me off — my phone vibrating, people talking out in the hall, or even just a thought like, “Oh, I forgot to do that thing for the league.” And then a few minutes are gone.\n\nWith reading, it’s more like I get to the end of a paragraph and realize I don’t really know what I just read. I saw the words, but I didn’t take them in. So I have to go back and read the same paragraph again, sometimes more than once, especially if there’s any distraction around me or if my mind starts chasing some unrelated thought. It’s annoying because I’m not trying to skim it or ignore it — I genuinely want to understand it — but I’ll catch myself after the fact and think, “Wait, I have no idea what that just said.”\n\nDoctor: Have there been long stretches when you felt down or depressed most days?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it “long stretches,” no. I mean, I’ve had periods where I felt worn down or discouraged, or just kind of low for a bit, especially when things feel like they’re piling up. But when you say depressed most days for a long stretch, I don’t immediately think, yes, that’s been me.\n\nI guess I’d want to clarify what you mean by long — like weeks, months? Because I can have bad days, or maybe a rough week where my mood isn’t great, but I don’t know that I’ve had a sustained period where I felt down or depressed most days in that more ongoing way.\n\nSo my best answer is: not really that I’ve noticed, at least not in a clear, prolonged way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

Because I can have bad days, or maybe a rough week where my mood isn’t great, but I don’t know that I’ve had a sustained period where I felt down or depressed most days in that more ongoing way.\n\nSo my best answer is: not really that I’ve noticed, at least not in a clear, prolonged way.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find yourself worrying excessively about many different things?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / medical_or_health_services_manager / associates\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so, not in the sense of worrying excessively about lots of different things all the time.

I mean, I worry about normal things — work stuff, money here and there, family things, whether I’m keeping up with responsibilities. But I wouldn’t say it feels like this constant, uncontrollable worry that jumps from topic to topic and takes over my day. At least, that’s not how I’d describe it.

Sometimes if something is unresolved, I’ll think about it more than I probably need to, especially if there’s a decision hanging over me or I’m waiting on an answer. But usually it’s tied to something specific, not just a broad sense of worry about everything.

So… no, I don’t think excessive worry across many different things is a major issue for me.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, thanks for seeing me. I guess the main thing is… I’m having a really hard time with anything that requires me to sit down and do sustained mental work. Like, at my job, I can handle a lot of moving parts in the moment, but when it comes to things like monthly reports or planning out a multi-site health event in a detailed, organized way, it’s like I hit a wall.\n\nI know the work matters. I’m not confused about the importance of it, and I’m not trying to avoid responsibility. But I keep putting those kinds of tasks off, sometimes until there’s real pressure, and the whole time it’s sitting in the back of my mind making me feel awful. It’s especially the stuff that takes focused brain effort for a long stretch — sitting there, sorting details, writing, planning, making decisions in sequence. I’ll mean to start, and then I just… don’t, or I start and stall out almost immediately.\n\nThat’s really what brought me in. It’s starting to feel like more than just being busy or not liking paperwork.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of trouble with schoolwork or homework when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah… yes, I think so. Looking back, it was there pretty early, even in elementary school. The way it was always described to my parents was, you know, “He’s bright, he understands the material,” but then there was always a “but.” Like, “but he’s daydreaming,” or “but he’s not keeping track of his papers,” or “but he didn’t turn in the worksheet.”\n\nI remember losing worksheets constantly — or not even losing them exactly, just having them disappear into my desk or backpack somehow. And homework was a whole production at home. My mother basically had to check my backpack every night, because if she didn’t, I’d forget something or not realize there was an assignment due, or I’d do it and then not turn it in. That happened enough that it became kind of a routine, her going through my folder and asking, “What’s this? Was this supposed to be handed in?” And half the time I’d be like, “Oh… yeah.”\n\nSo it wasn’t that I couldn’t learn the stuff. I think that’s what made it confusing for everyone. It was more that the follow-through — keeping the paper, remembering it, getting it back to school, actually handing it in — that whole chain was where things kept falling apart.\n\nDoctor: When you were a child, were you often fidgety or unable to stay seated when you were expected to?\n\nPatient: Hmm… that’s a little harder for me to say. I don’t really remember being the kid who was constantly jumping out of his seat or running around the classroom when I wasn’t supposed to. At least, that’s not the part that stands out in my memory, and I don’t remember teachers making that the main complaint.\n\nI mean, maybe I shifted around or doodled or messed with things at my desk like any kid, but I don’t have a clear memory of being unable to stay seated. The stuff I remember more is kind of different from that, but in terms of being physically fidgety or always up and moving, I’m not sure I could honestly say that was a big issue.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often feel restless, like it’s hard to relax or stay still inside?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that one. I don’t think of myself as physically restless in the obvious way, like I’m pacing all the time or unable to sit through a meeting. I mean, I can sit still if I need to.\n\nThe “inside” part is harder. Sometimes I feel mentally wound up, but I don’t know if that’s restlessness exactly or more just stress from knowing I’m behind on something or that I’ve got things hanging over me. I wouldn’t say relaxing comes naturally to me all the time, but I also don’t have a clear sense of being driven by a motor or needing to constantly move.\n\nSo… maybe a little at times, but it’s not one of the main things I would have thought to mention. I’d probably need you to define what you mean by restless, because I’m not sure if what I experience counts.\n\nDoctor: As an adult, do you often make careless mistakes or miss details in your work?\n\nPatient: Yes. That one I can answer pretty clearly. I do that more than I’d like, and it’s frustrating because a lot of the time I genuinely think I checked it.\n\nLike at work, I might send out a clinic staffing schedule and feel like I looked it over — names, dates, coverage, all of that — and then someone replies and says, “Did you mean Tuesday instead of Wednesday?” or “This person isn’t at that location,” or there’s a missing detail that should have been obvious. And it’s not because I don’t care. I really do care, especially with work stuff, because it affects other people. But somehow my eyes just kind of pass over the mistake.\n\nIt happens outside of work too. I help with emails for my soccer league sometimes, and I’ll send something out thinking it’s complete, and then somebody points out I put the wrong date, or I forgot to include the field location, or I left out some detail that everyone needs. And then I feel embarrassed because it’s the kind of thing where I think, “How did I not see that?” I mean, I had it right in front of me.\n\nSo, yeah, careless mistakes or missed details are definitely a thing for me. Not every single thing I do, but often enough that it’s become a pattern, and I’ve had to try to double-check more than once because one pass doesn’t always catch it.\n\nDoctor: When you’re trying to do a task that needs focus, do you often get distracted by your own thoughts or things around you?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. That happens a lot, and it’s one of those things that can be hard to explain because it’s not like I’m trying not to pay attention. I’ll be in a meeting, for example, and I’ll be making a real effort to follow what’s being said — like a budget update or some staffing-related discussion — and then something small pulls me off track.\n\nIt could be my phone vibrating, even if I don’t pick it up. Just feeling it or hearing it buzz can make my mind go, “What was that? Is that something I need to deal with?” Or if there are people talking in the hallway outside the conference room, I’ll suddenly realize I’m listening to them instead of the person in the meeting. And sometimes it’s not even something outside me. I’ll have a random thought pop in, like, “Oh, I forgot to send that thing for the soccer league,” and then my brain sort of follows that thread.\n\nBy the time I catch myself and come back to the meeting, I’ve missed a few minutes, and then I’m trying to piece together what was said from context. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. So yes, distractions from both around me and my own thoughts are a pretty regular problem when I’m trying to focus.\n\nDoctor: Do you have recurrent unwanted thoughts that feel intrusive and hard to dismiss?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say that, no. I mean, I have thoughts that get stuck sometimes, but usually they’re more like worries or reminders — like, “Did I forget to send that?” or “I need to follow up on this” — not thoughts that feel bizarre or scary or totally unwanted in that intrusive way.\n\nWhen you say “intrusive,” I think of something that just keeps forcing its way in and feels really disturbing or hard to shake, and I don’t think that’s a major thing for me. I can get mentally caught up in stuff, especially if there’s something unresolved, but it feels more like my mind jumping back to responsibilities than having unwanted thoughts I can’t dismiss.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: not really, or at least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do people often tell you that you seem not to listen when they’re speaking to you directly?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure, honestly. I don’t think people say that to me in those exact words very often, like “you’re not listening.” At least not that I can immediately think of.\n\nSometimes someone might check in and say, “Did you hear what I said?” or “Does that make sense?” but I don’t know if that’s because I seemed checked out or just because they wanted a response from me. I may need to think about that a little more, because I’m not always the best judge of how I come across in the moment.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say I’ve noticed people often telling me that directly, no. If anything, it’s more something I’d have to ask people close to me about to know for sure.\n\nDoctor: When you read something that you want to understand, do you often find yourself having to reread the same paragraph because your mind drifted off?\n\nPatient: Yes, that happens. Not every single time I read, but often enough that I notice it and it gets frustrating.\n\nI’ll be reading something I actually need to understand — like a report or an email with details I’m supposed to follow — and my eyes keep moving down the page, but my mind has kind of left. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment. Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHmm… that’s a little harder for me to say. I don’t really remember being the kid who was constantly jumping out of his seat or running around the classroom when I wasn’t supposed to. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. It’s embarrassing because I can be sitting there looking like I’m listening, and I want to be listening, but I’m not fully taking it in. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":["S002","S003","S008"],"denied":[],"reasoning":{"S002":"The patient describes regularly losing focus during meetings despite making a real effort to follow what is being said.","S003":"They report appearing to listen in meetings but not fully taking in what is being said after their attention drifts.","S008":"They clearly describe being pulled off track by external stimuli such as phone vibrations and hallway conversations, as well as by unrelated internal thoughts."}}

Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure I’d say that, no. I can get mentally caught up in stuff, especially if there’s something unresolved, but it feels more like my mind jumping back to responsibilities than having unwanted thoughts I can’t dismiss.\n\nSo I guess my answer would be: not really, or at least not in the way I think you mean.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.   }

Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not sure I’d call it “long stretches,” no. I mean, I’ve had periods where I felt worn down or discouraged, or just kind of low for a bit, especially when things feel like they’re piling up.
